# **Introduction**

This Web Annex provides information on the literature search and selection of clinical practice guidelines and Cochrane systematic reviews included in the _Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Module 2: musculoskeletal conditions (1)._ The methods applied for the search and selection are described in the Introduction to the _Package of interventions for rehabilitation (2)_ . The Web Annex further provides background information on selected guidelines and evidence tables for the interventions included in the health condition specific packages.

# **References**

1. Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Module 2. Musculoskeletal conditions. Geneva: World Health Organization; 2023 (Package of interventions for rehabilitation). Licence: CC BY-NC-SA 3.0 IGO.

2.  Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Module 1. Introduction. Geneva: World Health Organization; 2023 (Package of interventions for rehabilitation). Licence: CC BY-NC-SA 3.0 IGO.

### **Table A1.2. Searched non-academic databases**

#### **Guideline databases**

- Canadian Medical Association: CPG Infobase: Clinical Practice Guidelines

- Guidelines International Network

- American College of Physicians (ACP): Clinical Guidelines and Recommendations

- The Australian G overnment’s National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC)

- Health Services/Technology Assessment Texts (HSTAT)

- Institute for Clinical Systems Improvement (ICSI)

- National Institute for Health and Clinical Excellence (NICE) Guidance (United Kingdom)

- NICE Pathways

- New Zealand Guidelines Group

- Scottish Intercollegiate Guidelines Network (SIGN)

- World Health Organization guidelines

#### **Professional rehabilitation society websites**

- Italian Society of Physiatrists (SIMFER)

- Italian Society of Orthopaedics and Traumatology <u>(SIOT)</u>

- **Other**

- Index to Chiropractic Literature

- Trip Medical Database

- National Health Services Economic Evaluation Database (NHS EED)

**Table A1.6. Classification of the strength of recommendation in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Strength of recommendation**|||
|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**Strong**|**Weak/Conditional**|**Expert opinion/no recommendation**|**Note**|
|**NICE**(_1_)|**Strong:**For ‘strong’ recommendations on<br>interventions that ‘should’ be used, the guideline<br>development group is confident that, for the vast<br>majority of people, the intervention (or interventions)<br>will do more good than harm<br>**Strong against:**For ‘strong’ recommendations on<br>interventions that ‘should not’ be used, the guideline<br>development group is confident that, for the vast<br>majority of people, the intervention (or interventions)<br>will do more harm thangood.|**Conditional**:For ‘conditional’<br>recommendations on interventions that<br>should be ‘considered’, the guideline<br>development group is confident that the<br>intervention will do more good than harm for<br>most patients. The choice of intervention is<br>therefore more likely to vary depending on a<br>person’s values and preferences, and so the<br>healthcare professional should spend more<br>time discussingthe options with thepatient.|**Expert opinion:**Recommended best<br>practice based on the clinical<br>experience of the guideline<br>development group.|The wording of the evidence<br>statements reflects the certainty<br>or uncertainty in the estimate of<br>effect.|
|**APS**_(2)_|**A:**The panel strongly recommends that clinicians<br>consider offering the intervention to eligible patients.<br>The panel found good evidence that the intervention<br>improves health outcomes and concludes that<br>benefits substantially outweigh harms.<br>**D**The panel recommends**against**offering the<br>intervention. The panel found at least fair evidence<br>that the intervention is ineffective or that harms<br>outweigh benefits.|**B:**The panel recommends that clinicians<br>consider offering the intervention to eligible<br>patients. The panel found at least fair evidence<br>that the intervention improves health<br>outcomes and concludes that benefits<br>moderately outweigh harms, or that benefits<br>are small but there are no significant harms,<br>costs, or burdens associated with the<br>intervention.|<br>**C:**The panel makes no<br>recommendation for or against the<br>intervention. The panel found at least<br>fair evidence that the intervention can<br>improve health outcomes, but<br>concludes that benefits only slightly<br>outweigh harms, or the balance of<br>benefits and harms is too close to<br>justifyageneral recommendation.|-|
|**ICSI**_(3)_|**Strong**|**Weak**|**Consensus:**When there was a paucity<br>of systematic reviews and randomized<br>controlled trials, the work group used<br>the best available evidence to reach<br>consensus.|ICSI utilizes a modified version of<br>the Grading of Recommendations<br>Assessment, Development and<br>Evaluation (GRADE) methodology<br>system.|
|**CCGI**_(4)_|**Strong**|**Conditional**|**-**|Strength of each recommendation<br>was determined using informed<br>judgment on the quality of<br>evidence; balance of desirable and<br>undesirable outcomes; confidence<br>in the values and preferences for<br>the targetpopulation and costs.|
|**mhGAP**_(14)_|**Strong:**Recommendation suggests that the guideline<br>development group agreed that the quality of the<br>evidence combined with certainty about the values<br>and preferences and the feasibility of the<br>recommendation meant it should be followed in all or<br>almost all circumstances.|**Conditional:**Recommendation suggests less<br>certainty about the quality of evidence and<br>variation values and preferences and<br>feasibility, leading to circumstances in which<br>the recommendation may not apply.|**Good practice points:**Recommended<br>best practice based on the clinical<br>experience of the guideline<br>development group|-|

APS: American Pain Society _(2)_ ; CCGI: Canadian Chiropractic Guideline Initiative _(4)_ ; ICSI: Institute for Clinical Systems Improvement _(3)_ ; mhGAP: WHO Mental Health Gap Action Programme Intervention Guide _(14)_ ; NICE: National Institute for Health and Care Excellence _(1)_ .

### **Table A1.7. Classification of the quality of the evidence in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Quality of the**|**evidence**||
|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**High **|**Moderate**|**Low**|**Very low**|
|**NICE**(_1_)|**High:**Further research is very unlikely to<br>change our confidence in the estimate of<br>effect.|**Moderate:**Further research is likely to<br>have an important impact on our<br>confidence in the estimate of effect and<br>maychange the estimate.|**Low:**Further research is very likely to<br>have an important impact on our<br>confidence in the estimate of effect and<br>is likelyto change the estimate.|**Very low:**Any estimate of effect is very<br>uncertain.|
|**APS**_(2)_|**Good**: Evidence includes consistent results<br>from well designed, well-conducted<br>studies in representative populations that<br>directly assess effects on health outcomes<br>(at least 2 consistent, higher-quality trials).|**Fair:**Evidence is sufficient to determine<br>effects on health outcomes, but the<br>strength of the evidence is limited by the<br>number quality, size, or consistency of<br>included studies; generalizability to<br>routine practice; or indirect nature of the<br>evidence on health<br>outcomes (at least 1 higher-quality trial of<br>sufficient sample size; 2 or more higher<br>quality trials with some inconsistency; at<br>least 2 consistent, lower-quality trials, or<br>multiple consistent observational studies<br>with no significant methodologic flaws).|**Poor:**Evidence is insufficient to assess<br>effects on health outcomes because of<br>limited no; or<br>power of studies, large and unexplained<br>inconsistency between higher-quality<br>trials, important flaws in trial design or<br>conduct,<br>gaps in the chain of evidence, or lack of<br>information on important health<br>outcomes.|**-**|
|**ICSI**_(3)_|**High:**Further research is very unlikely to<br>change our confidence in the estimate of<br>effect.|**Moderate:**Further research is likely to<br>have an important impact on our<br>confidence in the Estimate of effect and<br>maychange the estimate.|**Low:**Further research is very likely to<br>have an important impact on our<br>confidence in the estimate of effect and<br>is likelyto change**t**he estimate.|**Very low:**Any estimate of effect is very<br>uncertain.|
|**CCGI**_(4)_|**High:**Further research is very unlikely to<br>change our confidence in the estimate of<br>effect.|**Moderate:**Further research is likely to<br>have an important impact on our<br>confidence in the Estimate of effect and<br>maychange the estimate.|**Low:**Further research is very likely to<br>have an important impact on our<br>confidence in the estimate of effect and<br>is likelyto change the estimate.|**Very low:**Any estimate of effect is very<br>uncertain.|
|**mhGAP**_(14)_|**High:**High confidence in the correlation<br>between true and estimated effect.|**Moderate:**Moderate confidence in the<br>estimated effect. It is possible that the<br>true effect is very different from the<br>estimated effect.|**Low:**Limited confidence in the<br>estimated effect. The true effect may<br>be very different from the estimated<br>effect.|**Very low:**Very little confidence in the<br>estimated effect. The true effect is very<br>probably different from the estimated<br>effect.|

|**Content of thought(cata**|**strophizing, fear avoidance beliefs, outcome expectations, kinesiophobia)**||||
|Assessment of content<br>of thought|Catastrophizing, fear avoidance beliefs, kinesiophobia and outcome expectations) impact the level of functioning in<br>many people with low back pain (LBP). The development group considers the assessment (including previous<br>screening) of these aspects related to thoughts as essential for the determination of intervention needs. To the<br>knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment. The cost-benefit ratio in<br>relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of interventions for<br>rehabilitation. Furthermore,the assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by people with LBP.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

||In patients with non-radicular LBP who do not respond to usual, non-interdisciplinary interventions, it is recommended<br>that clinicians consider intensive interdisciplinary rehabilitation with a cognitive/behavioural emphasis (strong<br>recommendation, high-quality evidence). |Assessment of mental<br>health (depression,<br>anxiety, emotional<br>distress)|Conducting an assessment for mental, neurological, and substance use (MNS) conditions involves the following steps.<br>First, the presenting complaint is explored, then a history is obtained including asking about past MNS issues, general<br>health problems, family MNS history, and psychosocial history. Observe the person (Mental Status Exam), establish a<br>differential diagnosis, and identify the MNS condition. As part of the assessment, conduct a physical examination and<br>obtain basic laboratorytests as needed. The assessment is conducted with informed consent of theperson.|n.a.|n.a.|mhGAP<br>_(14):_IG|
|Antidepressants|As first-line therapy, health care providers may select psychological treatments (such as behavioural activation,<br>cognitive behavioural therapy, or interpersonal psychotherapy or antidepressant medication (such as selective<br>serotonin reuptake inhibitors and tricyclic antidepressants. They should keep in mind the possible adverse effects<br>associated with antidepressant medications, the ability to deliver either intervention (in terms of expertise, and/or<br>treatment availability),and individualpreferences.|_C_onditional|Low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_DEP|
||Neither antidepressants nor benzodiazepines should be used for the initial treatment of individuals with complaints of<br>depressive symptoms in absence of current/prior depressive episode/disorder.|Strong|Low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_OTH|
|Psychological therapies<br>(incl. cognitive<br>behavioural therapy)|Health care providers can offerdifferent treatment formats of WHO’s recommended, structured psychological<br>interventions for adults and older adolescents with depressive disorder. These include behavioural activation,<br>cognitive behavioural therapy, interpersonal psychotherapy, problem-solving treatment as an adjunct treatment (e.g.<br>in combination with antidepressants). Different treatment formats for consideration include (a) individual and/or<br>group face-to-face psychological treatments delivered by professionals and supervised lay therapists, as well as (b)<br>self-help psychological treatment. While face-to-face psychological treatment or guided self-help psychological<br>treatment are likely to have better outcomes than unguided self-help, the latter may be suitable for those people who<br>either (a) do not have access to face-to-face psychological treatment or guided self-help psychological treatment or (b)<br>are not willingto access such treatments.|_C_onditional|Low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_DEP|
||Psychological treatment based on cognitive-behavioural therapy principles (such as reattribution, graded activities)<br>should be considered in repeat adult help seekers with medically unexplained somatic complaints who are in<br>substantial distress and who do not meet criteria for depressive episode/disorder.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_OTH|
||If it is possible to continue to follow-up the patient, graded self-exposure based on the principles of cognitive-<br>behavioural therapyshould be considered in adults withpost-traumatic stress disorder symptoms.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_OTH|
||Providing access to support based on the principles of psychological first aid should be considered for people in acute<br>distress exposed recentlyto a traumatic event.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_OTH|
||If human resources are available in non-specialized health care setting, a problem-solving approach should be<br>considered in people with depressive symptoms (in absence of depressive episode/disorder) - who are in distress or<br>have some degree of impaired functioning.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_OTH|
|Physical exercise<br>training|Advice on physical activity should be encouraged as part of treatment for adults with depressive episode/disorder with<br>inactive lifestyles. In moderate and severe depression, this intervention should be considered as adjunct to<br>antidepressants or brief structuredpsychological treatments.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_DEP|
|Stress management<br>training|In non-specialized health-care settings, relaxation training should be considered as treatment for anxiety symptoms (in<br>absence of depressive episode/disorder) (forpeople)who are in distress or have some degree of impaired functioning.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_OTH|
||Relaxation training may be considered as treatment of adults with depressive episode/disorder. In moderate and<br>severe depression, this intervention should be considered as an adjunct to antidepressants or structured brief<br>psychological treatments.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(14):_DEP|

a See Table A1.6 for explanation of the strength of recommendation categories. b See Table A1.7. for explanation of the quality of evidence categories. c See Table A1.5. for reference to the clinical practice guideline. ADL: activities of daily living; DG: development group; LBP: low back pain; MNS: mental, neurological, and substance use conditions; n.a.: not available; NHS: National Health Service; NSAIDs: non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs; SMT: spinal manipulative therapy.

|**Clinical practice**||**Strength of recommenda**|**tion**||
|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**Strong**|**Weak/Conditional**|**Expert opinion**|**Note**|
|**OARSI**_(5)_|**Strong for (Level 1A):**<br>**75-100% in favour, 0-25 against**<br>Voting panel members feel confident that the<br>benefits of a particular intervention outweigh<br>the harms.<br>**Strong against (Level 5):**<br>**0-25% in favour, 75-100 against**<br>Voting panel members feel confident that the<br>harms outweigh the benefits.|**Conditional:**If an intervention carries risks that<br>could potentially outweigh the benefits. A<br>higher degree of uncertainty in values and<br>preferences is more likely to result in a<br>conditional recommendation.<br>**Level 1B**75-100 in favour, 0-25 against<br>**Level 2**60-74 in favour, 26-40 against<br>**Level 3**41-59 in favour, 41-59 against<br>**Level 4B**26-40 in favour, 60-74 against<br>**Level 4A**0-25 in favour,75-100 against|**Good clinical practice statements:**<br>Recommendations were made<br>based on expert experience in the<br>absence of direct, supportive RCT<br>evidence.|-|
|**RACGP**_(6)_|**Strong for:**The working group is very confident<br>that the benefits of an intervention clearly<br>outweigh the harms.<br>**Strong against:**The working group is very<br>confident that the harms of an intervention<br>clearly outweigh the benefits.|**Conditional for:**Denotes uncertainty over the<br>balance of benefits, such as when the evidence<br>quality is low or very low, or when personal<br>preferences or costs are expected to impact the<br>decision, and as such refer to decisions where<br>consideration of personal preferences is<br>essential for decision making.<br>**Conditional against:**Denotes uncertainty over<br>the balance of harms, such as when the<br>evidence quality is low or very low, or when<br>personal preferences or costs are expected to<br>impact the decision, and as such refer to<br>decisions w here consideration of personal<br>preferences is essential for decision making.|**Conditional (neutral):**The working<br>group cannot determine the<br>direction of the recommendation.|-|
|**EULAR-a**_(9)_|**A:**Based on consistent level 1 evidence.<br>**B:**Based on consistent level 2 or 3 evidence or<br>extrapolations from level 1 evidence.|**C:**Based on level 4 evidence or extrapolations<br>from level 2 or 3 evidence.<br>**D:**Based on level 4 evidence or extrapolations<br>from level 2 or 3 evidence.|-|In addition to strength of<br>recommendation, there is also level<br>of agreement (LOA) to each of the<br>statements. LOA is a numerical rating<br>scale of 0-10 (0: do not agree at all,<br>10: fullyagree).|
|**EULAR-b**_(10)_|-|-|-|For each recommendation, only level<br>of agreement (LOA) is available which<br>a numerical rating scale of 0-10 is (0:<br>do not agree at all,10: fullyagree).|

|**Clinical practice**||**Strength of recommendat**|**ion**||
|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**Strong**|**Weak/Conditional**|**Expert opinion**|**Note**|
|**NICE**_(13)_|**Strong:**For ‘strong’ recommendations on<br>interventions that ‘should’ be used, the<br>guideline development group is confident that,<br>for the vast majority of people, the intervention<br>(or interventions) will do more good than harm.<br>**Strong against:**For‘strong’ recommendations<br>on interventions that ‘should not’ be used, the<br>guide-line development group is confident that,<br>for the vast majority of people, the intervention<br>(or interventions)will do more harm thangood.|**Conditional:** For ‘conditional’ recommendations<br>on interventions that should be ‘considered’,<br>the guideline development group is confident<br>that the intervention will do more good than<br>harm for most patients. The choice of<br>intervention is there- fore more likely to vary<br>depending on a person’svalues and<br>preferences, and so the healthcare professional<br>should spend more time discussing the options<br>with thepatient.|**Good practice points:**<br>Recommended best practice based<br>on the clinical experience of the<br>guideline development group.|-|
|**ACR**_(20)_|**Strong:**Typically, if panel members are very<br>certain that benefits do, or do not, outweigh<br>risks and burdens, they will make a strong<br>recommendation.|**Conditional:**If panel members decide that<br>benefits and risks/burdens are balanced, and/or<br>considerable uncertainty exists about the<br>magnitude of benefits and risks, they may make<br>a conditional<br>recommendation. In addition, when panel<br>members believe that fully informed patients<br>are likely to make different choices based on<br>their own values and preferences despite strong<br>evidence, they will likely offer a conditional<br>recommendation.|-|-|
|**mhGAP**_(37)_|**Strong:**Recommendation suggests that the<br>guideline development group agreed that the<br>quality of the evidence combined with certainty<br>about the values and preferences and the<br>feasibility of the recommendation meant it<br>should be followed in all or almost all<br>circumstances.|**Conditional:**Recommendation suggests less<br>certainty about the quality of evidence and<br>variation values and preferences and feasibility,<br>leading to circumstances in which the<br>recommendation may not apply.|**Good practice points:**<br>Recommended best practice based<br>on the clinical experience of the<br>guideline development group|-|

|**NICE**_(13)_|**High:**Further research is very<br>unlikely to change confidence<br>of DG in the estimate of<br>effect.|**Moderate:**Further research is likely<br>to have an important impact on our<br>confidence in the estimate of effect<br>and may change the estimate.|**Low:**Further research is very<br>likely to have an important<br>impact on our confidence in the<br>estimate of effect and is likely to<br>change the estimate.|**Very low:**Any estimate of effect is<br>very uncertain.|This guideline updates and replaces<br>NICE guideline CG59. ## **2.3 Cochrane systematic reviews included for osteoarthritis**

No Cochrane systematic review for osteoarthritis was included in this Web Annex.

## **2.4 Evidence table for osteoarthritis**

**Table A2.8. Evidence table for assessments and interventions included in the** **_Package of interventions for rehabilitation for osteoarthritis_**

|**Assessments and**<br>**interventions**|**Original recommendation from clinical practice guidelines/Development group members’rationale**|**Strength of**<br>**recommendation**<sup>a</sup>|**Quality of th**<br>**evidence**<sup>b</sup>|**e**<br>**Reference**<sup>c</sup>|
|---|---|---|---|---|
|**Energy and drive(fatigue**|**)**||||
|Assessment of fatigue|In people with hip or knee osteoarthritis (OA), initial assessments should use a biopsychosocial approach including:<br>a- physical status (including pain; fatigue; sleep quality; lower limb joint status (foot, knee, hip); mobility; strength; joint<br>alignment; proprioception and posture; comorbidities; weight) (…)|Level of<br>agreement<br>(LOA): 8.6 (7.9 to<br>9.2)|1b|EULAR-b<br>_(10)_: p.<br>1127; table<br>2.|
|**Content of thought(catas**|**trophizing, fear avoidance beliefs, kinesiophobia and outcome expectations)**||||
|Assessment of content<br>of thought<br>(catastrophizing, fear<br>avoidance beliefs,<br>kinesiophobia and<br>outcome expectations)|Catastrophizing, fear avoidance beliefs, kinesiophobia and outcome expectations impact the level of functioning in many<br>people with OA. The development group considers the assessment (including previous screening) of these aspects<br>related to thoughts as essential for the determination of intervention needs. To the knowledge of the development<br>group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits<br>justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the assessment<br>is considered as feasible and accepted by people with OA.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Cognitive behavioural<br>therapy|The development group considers cognitive behavioural therapy as an effective intervention to address problems<br>related to the content of thoughts. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with<br>cognitive behavioural therapy. ||Weight management.|Good clinical<br>practice<br>statement|n.a.|OARSI_(5)_: p.<br>5; table III.|

||Conducting an assessment for mental, neurological, and substance use (MNS) conditions involves the following steps.<br>First, the presenting complaint is explored, then a history is obtained including asking about past MNS issues, general<br>health problems, family MNS history, and psychosocial history. Observe the person (Mental Status Exam), establish a<br>differential diagnosis, and identify the MNS condition. As part of the assessment, conduct a physical examination and<br>obtain basic laboratorytests as needed. The assessment is conducted with informed consent of theperson.|n.a.|n.a.|mhGAP<br>_(37):_IG|
|Antidepressants|As first-line therapy, health care providers may select psychological treatments (such as behavioural activation, CBT, or<br>interpersonal psychotherapy) or antidepressant medication (such as selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors and tricyclic<br>antidepressants). They should keep in mind the possible adverse effects associated with antidepressant medications,<br>the ability to deliver either intervention (in terms of expertise, and/or treatment availability), and individual<br>preferences.|_C_onditional|Low|mhGAP<br>_(37):_DEP|
||Neither antidepressants nor benzodiazepines should be used for the initial treatment of individuals with complaints of<br>depressive symptoms in absence of current/prior depressive episode/disorder.|Strong|Low|mhGAP<br>_(37):_OTH|
|Psychological therapies<br>(incl. cognitive<br>behavioural therapy)|It may be appropriate to offer CBT for some people with knee and/or hip OA. Clinicians should consider whether CBT is<br>appropriate, taking into account psychological comorbidities and personal preference. They should be cognisant of<br>issues related to cost and access. It is recommended that CBT is combined with exercise to improve outcomes. CBT may<br>be offered face-to-face or via onlineprograms.|Conditional for|Knee: low<br>Hip: very low|RACGP_(6)_:<br>p. 27; 3.1.2.|

||Health care providers can offer different treatment formats of WHO’s recommended, structured psychological<br>interventions for adults and older adolescents with depressive disorder. These include behavioural activation, CBT,<br>interpersonal psychotherapy, problem-solving treatment as an adjunct treatment (e.g. in combination with<br>antidepressants). Different treatment formats for consideration include (a) individual and/or group face-to-face<br>psychological treatments delivered by professionals and supervised lay therapists, as well as (b) self-help psychological<br>treatment. While face-to-face psychological treatment or guided self-help psychological treatment are likely to have<br>better outcomes than unguided self-help, the latter may be suitable for those people who either (a) do not have access<br>to face-to-face psychological treatment or guided self-help psychological treatment or (b) are not willing to access such<br>treatments.|_C_onditional|Low|mhGAP<br>_(37):_DEP|
||Psychological treatment based on cognitive-behavioural therapy principles (such as reattribution, graded activities)<br>should be considered in repeat adult help seekers with medically unexplained somatic complaints who are in<br>substantial distress and who do not meet criteria for depressive episode/disorder.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(37):_OTH|
||If it is possible to continue to follow-up the patient, graded self-exposure based on the principles of cognitive -<br>behavioural therapyshould be considered in adults withpost-traumatic stress disorder symptoms.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(37):_OTH|
||Providing access to support based on the principles of psychological first aid should be considered for people in acute<br>distress exposed recentlyto a traumatic event.|Conditional|Very low|mhGAP<br>_(37):_OTH|

### **Table A3.6. Classification of the strength of recommendation in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Strength of recommendation**|||
|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**Strong**|**Weak/Conditional**|**Expert opinion**|**Note**|
|**HAS**_(1)_|**A:**Based on level of evidence 1.<br>**B:**Based on level of evidence 2.|**C:**Based on level of evidence 3 or 4.|When no grade could be allocated,<br>working group members<br>attempted to reach**professional**<br>**agreement(PA)**.|-|
|**EULAR**_(2)_|**A:**Based on category 1 evidence.<br>**B:**Based on category 2 evidence or extrapolations from<br>category 1 evidence.|**C:**Based on category 3 evidence or extrapolations<br>from category 1 or 2 evidence.<br>**D:**Based on category 4 evidence or extrapolations<br>from category2 or 3 evidence.|**-**|-|
|**NICE**_(3)_|**Strong:**For ‘strong’ recommendations on interventions<br>that ‘should’ be used, the guideline development group<br>is confident that, for the vast majority of people, the<br>intervention (or interventions) will do more good than<br>harm.**Strong:**For ‘strong’ recommendations on<br>interventions that ‘should not’ be used, the guideline<br>development group is confident that, for the vast<br>majority of people, the intervention (or interventions)<br>will do more harm thangood.|**Conditional:** For ‘conditional’ recommendations<br>on interventions that should be ‘considered’, the<br>guideline development group is confident that the<br>intervention will do more good than harm for<br>most patients. The choice of intervention is<br>therefore more likely to vary depending on a<br>person’s values and preferences, and so the<br>healthcare professional should spend more time<br>discussingthe options with thepatient.|**Good practice points:**<br>Recommended best practice based<br>on the clinical experience of the<br>guideline development group.|-|
|**SPANISH**_(4)_|**A:**Based on level of evidence 1a, 1b or|**B:**Based on level of evidence 2a-c, 3a-b.<br>**C:**Based on level of evidence 4.|**D:**Expert opinion without explicit<br>critical appraisal, or based on<br>physiology, bench research or “first<br>principles”.|-|
|**SIGN**_(5)_|**A:**At least one meta-analysis, systematic review, or<br>randomized controlled trial rated as 1++, and directly<br>applicable to the target population; or a body of<br>evidence consisting principally of studies rated as 1+,<br>directly applicable to the target population, and<br>demonstrating overall consistency of results.<br>**B:**A body of evidence including studies rated as 2++,<br>directly applicable to the target population, and<br>demonstrating overall consistency of results; or<br>extrapolated evidence from studies rated as 1++ or 1+.|Extrapolated evidence from studies rated as 2++.<br>**C:**A body of evidence including studies rated as<br>2+, directly applicable to the target population<br>and demonstrating overall consistency of results.<br>**D:**Evidence level 3 or 4; or<br>extrapolated evidence from studies rated as 2+.|**Good practice point:**<br>Recommended best practice based<br>on the clinical experience of the<br>guideline development group.|The grade of<br>recommendation relates to<br>the strength of the evidence<br>on which the<br>recommendation is based. It<br>does not reflect the clinical<br>importance of the<br>recommendation.|
|**mhGAP**_(14)_|**Strong:**Recommendation suggests that the guideline<br>development group agreed that the quality of the<br>evidence combined with certainty about the values and<br>preferences and the feasibility of the recommendation<br>meant it should be followed in all or almost all<br>circumstances.|**Conditional:**Recommendation suggests less<br>certainty about the quality of evidence and<br>variation values and preferences and feasibility,<br>leading to circumstances in which the<br>recommendation may not apply.|**Good practice points:**<br>Recommended best practice based<br>on the clinical experience of the<br>guideline development group.|-|

**Table A3.7. Classification of the quality of the evidence in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**<br>||**Quality of the evide**|**nce**|||
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**High **|**Moderate**|**Low**|**Very low**|**Note**|
|**HAS**_(1)_|**1:**Trials of a high level of evidence<br>e.g. high power randomized<br>controlled trials (RCTs) free of<br>major bias and/or meta-analyses of<br>RCTs or decision analyses based on<br>level1 trials.|**2:**Studies of an intermediate level of<br>evidence e.g. RCTs with some bias, meta-<br>analyses based on questionable<br>methodology, well-conducted non-<br>randomized controlled trials or cohort<br>studies.|**3:**Studies of a lower level of<br>evidence, e.g. case control studies.<br>**4:**Case series.|-|-|
|**EULAR**_(2)_|**1a:**Meta-analysis of RCTs.<br>**1b:**At least one RCT.|**2a:**At least one controlled study without<br>randomization.<br>**2b:**At least one type of quasi-experimental<br>study.|**3:**Descriptive studies, such as<br>comparative studies, correlation<br>studies, or case control studies.|**4:**Expert committee reports<br>or opinions and/or clinical<br>experience of respected<br>authorities.|-|
|**NICE**_(3)_|**High:**Further research is very<br>unlikely to change our confidence<br>in the estimate of effect.<br>**1++:**High-quality meta-analyses,<br>systematic reviews of RCTs, or<br>RCTs with a very low risk of bias.<br>**1+:**Well-conducted meta-analyses,<br>systematic reviews of RCTs, or<br>RCTs with a low risk of bias.|**Moderate:**Further research is likely to have<br>an important impact on our confidence in<br>the estimate of effect and may change the<br>estimate.<br>**1-:**Meta-analyses, systematic reviews of<br>RCTs, or RCTs with a high risk of bias.<br>**2++:**High-quality systematic reviews of case-<br>control or cohort studies. High-quality case-<br>control or cohort studies with a very low risk<br>of confounding or bias and a high probability<br>that the relationshipis causal.|**Low:**Further research is very likely<br>to have an important impact on<br>our confidence in the estimate of<br>effect and is likely to change the<br>estimate.<br>**-2:**Case-control or cohort studies<br>with a high risk of confounding,<br>bias or chance and a significant risk<br>that the relationship is not causal.<br>**3:**Non-analytic studies (for<br>example case reports,case series).|**Very low:**Any estimate of<br>effect is very uncertain.<br>**4:**Expert opinion, formal<br>consensus.|Because this guideline<br>updates and replaced<br>NICE guideline CG79,<br>different systems<br>have been used to<br>classify the quality of<br>evidence.|
|**SPANISH**_(4)_|**1a:**Systematic review of RCTs<br>(with homogeneity).<br>**1b:**Individual RCT (with narrow<br>confidence interval).<br>**1c:**“All or none” RCT.|**2a:**Systematic review (with homogeneity) of<br>cohort studies.<br>**2b:**Individual cohort study (or low quality<br>randomized controlled trial; e.g. <80%<br>follow-up).**2c:**"Outcomes" research;<br>ecological studies. <br>**3a:**Systematic review (with homogeneity) of<br>case-control studies.<br>**3b:**Individual case control study.|**4:**Case series (and poor quality,<br>cohort or case control studies).|**5:**Expert opinion without<br>explicit critical appraisal, or<br>based on physiology, bench<br>research or “first principles”.|-|
|**SIGN**_(5)_|**1++:**High quality meta-analyses,<br>systematic reviews of RCTs, or<br>RCTs with a very low risk of bias.<br>**1+:**Well conducted meta-analyses,<br>systematic reviews, or RCTs with a<br>low risk of bias.|**1-:**Meta-analyses, systematic reviews, or<br>RCTs with a high risk of bias.<br>**2++:**High quality systematic reviews of case<br>control or cohort studies, High quality case<br>control or cohort studies with a very low risk<br>of confounding or bias and a high probability<br>that the relationship is causal.|**2+:**Well conducted case control or<br>cohort studies with a low risk of<br>confounding or bias and a<br>moderate probability that the<br>relationship is causal.<br>**2-:**Case control or cohort studies<br>with a high risk of confounding or<br>bias and a significant risk that the<br>relationshipis not causal.|**3:**Non-analytic studies, e.g.<br>case reports, case series.<br>**4:**Expert opinion.|-|

|Assessment of fatigue|Fatigue presents a frequent problem in people with rheumatoid arthritis (RA). The development group considers the<br>assessment of fatigue as essential for the determination of intervention needs. ### **Table A4.6. Classification of the strength of recommendation in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Strength of recommendation**||
|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**Strong**|**Weak/Conditional**|**No recommendation**|
|**ICFSR**_(1)_|**High:**High confidence in the correlation between true and<br>estimated effect.|**Conditional:**The treatment had considerable undesirable<br>outcomes (such as patient burden, unwanted side effects,<br>and risk of adverse clinical outcomes) which undermined<br>the health benefits of the treatment–that is, whilst many<br>health practitioners would choose this treatment<br>modality,manywould not.|**No recommendation:**When insufficient evidence<br>existed to support any recommendation.|
|**CGS**_(2)_|**Strong:**When the desirable effects of an intervention clearly<br>outweigh the undesirable effects, or clearly do not, guideline<br>panels offer strong recommendations.|**Weak:**When the trade-offs are less certain - either<br>because of low quality evidence or because evidence<br>suggests that desirable and undesirable effects are closely<br>balanced - weak recommendations become mandatory|-|

## **4.4 Evidence table for sarcopenia**

**Table A4.8. Evidence table for assessments and interventions included in the** **_Package of interventions for rehabilitation for sarcopenia_**

|**Assessments and**<br>**interventions**|**Originalrecommendation from clinical practice guidelines/Development group members’ rationale**|**Strength of**<br>**recommendatio**|**n**<sup>a</sup><br>**Quality of the**<br>**evidence**<sup>b</sup>|<br>**Reference**<sup>c</sup>|
|---|---|---|---|---|
|**Prevention of malnutrition**|||||
|Assessment of nutritional<br>status|Malnutrition (specifically nutrient deficiencies) is associated with sarcopenia and an increased risk of morbidity and<br>mortality and is thus relevant to people with age-related sarcopenia. The development group considers the assessment<br>of nutritional status as essential for the determination of intervention needs. To the knowledge of the development<br>group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment of nutritional status. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to<br>the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation.<br>Furthermore,the assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by people with sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Nutritional management|The GDG recommends that clinicians consider protein supplementation/a protein-rich diet for older adults with<br>sarcopenia.|Conditional|Low|ICSFR_(1):_p.<br>1154; rec. 4.|
||Nutritional (protein) intervention should be combined with a physical activity intervention.|Conditional|Low|ICSFR_(1):_p.<br>1155;rec. 4.|
|**Muscle functions**|||||
|Assessment of muscle<br>functions|Reduced muscle strength and muscle mass is a frequent problem in people with age-related sarcopenia. The<br>development group considers the assessment of muscle functions as essential for the determination of intervention<br>needs. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment of muscle<br>functions. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the<br>Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by<br>people with sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Muscle-strengthening<br>exercises|In patients with sarcopenia, prescription of resistance-based training can be effective to improve muscle strength,<br>skeletal muscle mass andphysical function|Strong|Moderate|ICSFR_(1):_p.<br>1153; rec. 3.|
||Exercise interventions can be effective for increasing appendicular skeletal muscle mass, knee extension muscle<br>strength,normalgait speed and maximumgait speed inpatients with sarcopenia,and therefore are recommended.|Weak|Very low|CGS_(2):_p.<br>28; CQ1.|
|**Balance**|||||
|Assessment of balance|Impaired balance presents a frequent problem in people with age-related sarcopenia because it increases the risk for<br>falling. The development group considers the assessment of balance as essential for the determination of intervention<br>needs. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment of balance.<br>The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of<br>interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by people with<br>sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Balance training|Exercise interventions can be effective for increasing appendicular skeletal muscle mass, knee extension muscle<br>strength,normalgait speed and maximumgait speed inpatients with sarcopenia,and therefore are recommended.|Weak|Very low|CGS_(2):_p.<br>28; CQ1.|
|**Walking**|||||
|Assessment of (gait<br>pattern) and walking|The capacity to walk is often limited in people with age-related sarcopenia and associated with an increased risk for<br>falling. The development group considers the assessment of walking as essential for the determination of intervention<br>needs. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment of walking.<br>The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefitsjustifies the inclusion of the assessment of walkingin the|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

|**Mobility**|||||
|Provision and training in<br>the use of assistive<br>products for mobility|People with severe age-related sarcopenia often face problems with walking and, respectively, walking is associated<br>with a high risk for falling. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to<br>the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the provision and training in the use of assistive products for mobility in<br>the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the provision of these assistive products is considered as<br>feasible and accepted by people with severe sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

|Assessment of exercise<br>capacity|Reduced exercise capacity presents a frequent problem in people with age-related sarcopenia. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with<br>the assessment of exercise capacity. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of<br>the assessment in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the assessment is considered as<br>feasible and accepted by people with sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

Furthermore,the assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by people with sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with<br>ADL training. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of this intervention in the<br>Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the intervention is considered as feasible and accepted by<br>people with sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Modification of the home<br>environment|The capacity to perform activities of daily living safely is often limited in people with severe age-related sarcopenia due<br>to (amongst others) physical barriers. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with modification of the<br>environment. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of environmental<br>modification in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, environmental modification is considered<br>as feasible and accepted by people with sarcopenia.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the intervention. ### **Table A5.6. Classification of the strength of recommendation in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Strength of recommendation**||
|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**Strong**|**Weak/Conditional**|**Expert opinion/No recommendation**|
|**AAOS-a**_(1)_|**Strong:**Evidence is based on two or more “High”<br>strength studies with consistent findings for<br>recommending for or against the intervention.<br>A Strong recommendation means that the benefits of<br>the recommended approach clearly exceed the<br>potential harm (or that the potential harm clearly<br>exceeds the benefits in the case of a strong negative<br>recommendation), and that the strength of the<br>supporting evidence is high.|**Moderate:**Evidence from two or more“moderate” strength studies<br>with consistent findings, or evidence from a single “high” quality study<br>for recommending for or against the intervention. A moderate<br>recommendation means that the benefits exceed the potential harm<br>(or that the potential harm clearly exceeds the benefits in the case of<br>a negative recommendation), but the strength of the supporting<br>evidence is not as strong.<br>**Limited:**Evidence from two or more “low” strength studies with<br>consistent findings, or evidence from a single moderate quality study<br>recommending for or against the intervention or diagnostic. A limited<br>recommendation means the quality of the supporting evidence that<br>exists is unconvincing,or that well-conducted studies.|**Consensus:**The supporting evidence is lacking<br>and requires the work group to make a<br>recommendation based on expert opinion by<br>considering the known potential harm and<br>benefits associated with the treatment.<br>**Inconclusive:**Evidence from a single low-<br>quality study or conflicting findings that do<br>not allow a recommendation for or against<br>the intervention. An inconclusive<br>recommendation means that there is a lack of<br>compelling evidence resulting in an unclear<br>balance between benefits andpotential harm.|
|**DANISH**_(2)_|**Strong for:**The desirable consequences of an<br>intervention are judged to clearly outweigh<br>undesirable consequences.<br>**Strong against:**The undesirable consequences of an<br>intervention clearly outweigh desirable consequences.<br>Also, when the review of the evidence shows with<br>great certainty that an intervention is useless.|**Weak/conditional for:**Desirable consequences of an intervention are<br>judged to marginally outweigh undesirable consequences or when the<br>available evidence cannot rule out a significant benefit of an existing<br>practice if the adverse effects of the latter are judged to be few or<br>absent.<br>**Weak/conditional against:**The undesirable consequences of an<br>intervention are judged to outweigh desirable consequences, and this<br>is unsupported by strong evidence. This recommendation is also made<br>in case of strong evidence of both beneficial and adverse effects when<br>the balance between them is difficult to determine.|**Good practice:**Based on professional<br>consensus among the members of the<br>working group. The recommendation may be<br>either for or against the intervention. A good<br>practice recommendation is made when<br>relevant evidence is not available.|
|**NICE**_(3)_|**Strong:**For ‘strong’ recommendations on interventions<br>that ‘should’ be used, the guideline development<br>group is confident that, for the vast majority of people,<br>the intervention (or interventions) will do more good<br>than harm.<br>**Strong against:**For ‘strong’ recommendations on<br>interventions that ‘should not’ be used, the guideline<br>development group is confident that, for the vast<br>majority of people, the intervention (or interventions)<br>will do more harm thangood.|**Conditional:** For ‘conditional’ recommendations on interventions that<br>should be ‘considered’, the guideline development group is confident<br>that the intervention will do more good than harm for most patients.|**Expert opinion:**Recommended best practice<br>based on the clinical experience of the<br>guideline development group.|

### **Table A5.7. Classification of the quality of the evidence in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Quality of t**|**he evidence**|||
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**High **|**Moderate**|**Low**|**Very low**|**Note**|
|**AAOS-a**_(1)_**and**<br>**AAOS-b**_(6)_|**Level I:**High quality randomized<br>controlled trial (RCT) with<br>statically significant difference or<br>no statically significant difference<br>but narrow confidence interval.<br>Systematic review of level I RCTs.|**Level II:**Lesser quality RCTs,<br>prospective comparative studies,<br>systematic review of level II<br>studies or level I studies with<br>inconsistent results.<br>**Level III:**Case-control studies,<br>retrospective comparable studies,<br>systematic review of level III<br>studies.|**Level IV:**Case series<br>**Level V:**Expert opinion|-|The development group tied<br>quality to levels of evidence.|
|**DANISH**_(2)_|**High:**The guideline development<br>group were very confident that<br>the true effect lies close to that of<br>the estimate of the effect.|**Moderate:**The guideline<br>development group were<br>moderately confident in the effect<br>estimate: The true effect is likely<br>to be close to the estimate of the<br>effect, but there is a possibility<br>that it is substantially<br>different.|**Low:**The guideline development<br>group has confidence in the effect<br>estimate was limited: The true<br>effect may be substantially<br>different from the estimate of the<br>effect.|**Very low:**The guideline<br>development group has very little<br>confidence in the effect estimate:<br>The true effect is likely to be<br>substantially different from the<br>estimate of the effect.|-|
|**NICE**_(3)_|**High:**Further research is very<br>unlikely to change our confidence<br>in the estimate of effect.|**Moderate:**Further research is<br>likely to have an important impact<br>on our confidence in the estimate<br>of effect and may change the<br>estimate.|**Low:**Further research is very likely<br>to have an important impact on<br>our confidence in the estimate of<br>effect and is likely to change the<br>estimate.|**Very low:**Any estimate of effect is<br>very uncertain.|-|

AAOS-a: American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons _(1)_ ; AAOS-b: American Academy of Orthopaedic Surgeons _(6)_ ; DANISH: Danish Health Authority _(2)_ ; NICE: National Institute for Health and Care Excellence _(3)_ ; RCT: randomized controlled trial.

Furthermore, the assessment is<br>considered as feasible and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

To the knowledge of the development group, no<br>severe harms are associated with the prescription of oral NSAIDs (paracetamol) under the guidance of a rehabilitation<br>professional. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of oral NSAIDs<br>(paracetamol) in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the prescription of oral NSAIDs<br>(paracetamol)is considered as feasible and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

The development group considers the<br>provision and training in the use of orthoses as an effective intervention to address movement-related pain. To the<br>knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the provision of orthoses. The cost-benefit<br>ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of interventions for<br>rehabilitation. Furthermore, the provision and training in the use of orthoses is considered as feasible and accepted by<br>people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

|Assessment of hand and<br>arm use|Limitations in using the hand and arm are frequent in people with fractures in the upper extremity. The development<br>group considers the assessment of hand and arm use as essential for the determination of intervention needs. To the<br>knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment of hand and arm use. The<br>cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of<br>interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by people with<br>fractures in the upper extremity.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Functional training for<br>hand and arm use|Limitations in using the hand and arm are frequent in people with fractures in the upper extremity. The development<br>group considers functional training for hand and arm use as an effective intervention to address these limitations. To<br>the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with functional training for hand and arm<br>use. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the intervention in the Package of<br>interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, functional training for hand and arm use is considered as feasible and<br>accepted by people with fractures in the lower extremity.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|**Activities of daily living (AD**|**L)**||||
|Assessment of activities of<br>daily living|Activities of daily living (ADL) are often limited in people with fractures.The development group considers the<br>assessment of ADL as essential and, consequently, to consider the assessment result for the determination of<br>intervention needs. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the assessment<br>of ADL. Furthermore, the assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by people with<br>fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|ADL training|Performing ADL is often limited in people with fractures. Therefore, the development group considers ADL training as<br>essential. Furthermore,the intervention is considered as feasible and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Provision and training in<br>the use of assistive<br>products for self-care|Performing ADL is often limited in people with fractures. The development group considers the provision and training<br>in the use of assistive products for self-care as an effective intervention to address these limitations. To the knowledge<br>of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the provision of assistive products. The cost-benefit<br>ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of interventions for<br>rehabilitation. Furthermore, the provision of assistive products is considered as feasible and accepted by people with<br>fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Modification of the home<br>environment|The capacity to perform ADL is often limited in people with fractures due to physical barriers. The development group<br>considers environmental modification as an effective intervention to facilitate accessibility and thus, performing<br>activities of daily living. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with<br>modification of the environment. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of<br>environmental modification in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, environmental<br>modification is considered as feasible and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|**Work and employment**|||||
|Vocational assessment|The capacity to work and the performance of occupational tasks is often limited in people with fractures. The<br>development group considers vocational assessment as essential for the determination of intervention needs. To the<br>knowledge of the developmentgroup,no severe harms are associated with vocational assessment. The cost-benefit|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

|---|---|---|---|---|
|Vocational counselling,<br>training and support|Return to or maintenance at work often presents problems for people with fractures. The development group<br>considers vocational counselling, training, and support as an effective intervention to support return to or<br>maintenance at work. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the<br>intervention. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the intervention in the<br>Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, vocational counselling, training, and support is considered as<br>feasible and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|**Family and carer support**|||||
|Carer and family training<br>and support|There is an increased burden for caregivers when they need to support people with fractures. Therefore, the<br>development group considers carer and family training and support as essential. To the knowledge of the development<br>group, no severe harms are associated with caregiver training. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits<br>justifies the inclusion of this intervention in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, carer and<br>familytrainingand support is considered as feasible and accepted by people caringforpeople with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|**Self-management**|||||
|Education and advice on<br>self-directed exercise|A home exercise programme is an option for patients prescribed therapy after distal radius fracture.|Limited|Level II|AAOS-a_(1)_:<br>p. 84; 21.|
|Education and advice on<br>self-directed exercises|It is good practice not to prescribe rehabilitation supervised by an occupational therapist or a physiotherapist on a<br>routine basis to patients with uncomplicated cases. This is due to finding no difference in the effect as compared to<br>independent rehabilitation based on a written training plan following a single instruction. As a minimum, it is good<br>practice to offer guidance and practical instruction concerning self-rehabilitation following distal radial fracture to all<br>patients regardless of the treatment method.|Good practice|Very low|DANISH_(2)_:<br>p. 46; 7.2.|
|**Prevention ofpneumonia**|||||
|Assessment of respiratory<br>functions|People with fractures who are bedridden due to the fracture have an increased risk for pneumonia.The development<br>group considers the assessment of respiratory functions as essential and, consequently, to consider the assessment<br>result for the determination of intervention needs. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are<br>associated with the assessment of respiratory functions. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits<br>justifies the inclusion of the assessment in the Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the<br>assessment is considered as feasible and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|Breathing exercises|People with fractures who are bedridden due to the fracture have an increased risk for pneumonia. The development<br>group considers breathing exercises for those with an increased risk for pneumonia as an effective intervention. To the<br>knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with the breathing exercises. The cost-benefit<br>ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of the intervention in the Package of interventions for<br>rehabilitation. Furthermore,the intervention is considered as feasible and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|
|**Prevention of deep venous**|**thromboembolism**||||
|Anticoagulants|The risk for developing deep venous thrombosis (DVT) in people with fractures in the lower extremity is increased due<br>to reduced blood flow. The development group considers the prescription of anticoagulants as essential for the<br>prevention of DVT. To the knowledge of the development group, no severe harms are associated with receiving<br>anticoagulants. The cost-benefit ratio in relation to the clinical benefits justifies the inclusion of anticoagulants in the<br>Package of interventions for rehabilitation. Furthermore, the administration of anticoagulants is considered as feasible<br>and accepted by people with fractures.|n.a.|n.a.|DG expert<br>opinion|

The working group contacted the authors of this specific guideline for additional information concerning the conflict of interest and funding (item 22 of the AGREE II tool). This exception was made because this was the only remaining guideline concerning upper limp amputation and there were no other exclusion criteria except the score on item 22. The response of the authors was considered sufficient, which led to inclusion of the guideline.

### **Table A6.6. Classification of the strength of recommendation in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Strength of recomme**|**ndation**|||
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**Strong**|**Weak/Conditional**|**Expert opinion**||**Against**|
|**BACPAR**_(1)_|**A:**At least one meta-analysis, systematic<br>review, or randomized controlled trial rated as<br>1++, and directly applicable to the target<br>population; OR A body of evidence consisting<br>principally of studies rated as 1+, directly<br>applicable to the target population, and<br>demonstrating overall consistency of results.<br>**B:**A body of evidence including studies rated as<br>2++, directly applicable to the target<br>population, and demonstrating overall<br>consistency of results; or extrapolated evidence<br>from studies rated as 1++ or 1+.|**C:**A body of evidence including studies<br>rated as 2+, directly applicable to the target<br>population and demonstrating overall<br>consistency of results; or Extrapolated<br>evidence from studies rated as 2++.<br>**D:**Evidence level 3 or 4; or Extrapolated<br>evidence from studies rated as 2+.|-||-|
|**DVA/DoD**<br>**LowLimb**_(2)_|**Strong for:**The work group is highly confident<br>that desirable outcomes outweigh undesirable<br>outcomes.<br>**Strong against:**The work group is highly<br>confident that the undesirable consequences<br>outweigh the desirable consequences.|**Weak for:**The work group is less confident<br>of the balance between desirable and<br>undesirable outcomes.<br>**Weak against:**The work group is less<br>confident that the undesirable<br>consequences outweigh the desirable<br>consequences.|-||-|
|**DUTCH**_(3)_|**1:**One study of level A1 or at least two<br>independently conducted studies of level A2,<br>with consistent results.|**2:**One study of level A2 or at least two<br>independently conducted studies of level B.<br>**3:**One studyof level B or C.|**4:**Expert opinion.|||
|**DVA/DoD**<br>**UppLimb**_(5)_|**A:**There is high certainty that the net benefit is<br>substantial. ### **Table A6.7. Classification of the quality of the evidence in the included clinical practice guidelines**

|**Clinical practice**||**Quality of the**|**evidence**|||
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**guideline**|**High **|**Moderate**|**Low**|**Very low**|**Note**|
|**BACPAR**_(1)_|**1++:**High quality meta-analyses,<br>systematic reviews of randomized<br>controlled trials (RCTs), or RCTs<br>with a very low risk of bias.<br>**1+:**Well conducted meta-analyses,<br>systematic reviews, or RCTs with a<br>low risk of bias.<br>**1-:**Meta-analyses, systematic<br>reviews, or RCTs with a high risk of<br>bias.<br>**Ia:**Evidence obtained from<br>systematic review of randomized<br>controlled trial.<br>**Ib:**Evidence obtained from at least<br>one randomized controlled trial.|**2++:**High quality systematic reviews<br>of case control or cohort studies,<br>High quality case control or cohort<br>studies with a very low risk of<br>confounding or bias and a high<br>probability that the relationship is<br>causal.<br>**2+:**Well conducted case control or<br>cohort studies with a low risk of<br>confounding or bias and a moderate<br>probability that the relationship is<br>causal.<br>**2**-: Case control or cohort studies<br>with a high risk of confounding or<br>bias and a significant risk that the<br>relationship is not causal.<br>**IIa:**Evidence obtained from at least<br>one well designed controlled trial<br>without randomization.<br>**IIb:**Evidence obtained from at least<br>other type of well-designed quasi<br>experimental study.|**3:**Non-analytic studies, e.g. case<br>reports, case series.<br>**III:**Evidence obtained from well-<br>designed nonexperimental<br>descriptive studies, such as<br>comparative studies, correlation<br>studies and case studies.|**4:**Expert opinion.<br>**IV:**Evidence obtained from<br>expert committee reports or<br>opinions and/or clinical<br>experience of respected<br>authorities.|**Note:**The BACPAR<br>guidelines uses two<br>different systems for the<br>categorization of the quality<br>of the evidence.|

|Assessment of mental<br>health|Conducting an assessment for mental, neurological, and substance use (MNS) conditions involves the following steps.<br>First, the presenting complaint is explored, then a history is obtained including asking about past MNS issues, general<br>health problems, family MNS history, and psychosocial history. As part of the assessment, conduct a physical examination and<br>obtain basic laboratorytests as needed. The assessment is conducted with informed consent of theperson.|n.a.|n.a.|mhGAP (7): IG|

||Neither antidepressants nor benzodiazepines should be used for the initial treatment of individuals with complaints of<br>depressive symptoms in absence of current/prior depressive episode/disorder.|Strong|Low|mhGAP (7):<br>OTH|

|therapy)|Health care providers can offer different treatment formats of WHO’s recommended, structured psychological<br>interventions for adults and older adolescents with depressive disorder. These include behavioural activation, CBT,<br>interpersonal psychotherapy, problem-solving treatment as an adjunct treatment (e.g. in combination with<br>antidepressants). Different treatment formats for consideration include (a) individual and/or group face-to-face<br>psychological treatments delivered by professionals and supervised lay therapists, as well as (b) self-help psychological<br>treatment. While face-to-face psychological treatment or guided self-help psychological treatment are likely to have<br>better outcomes than unguided self-help, the latter may be suitable for those people who either (a) do not have access<br>to face-to-face psychological treatment or guided self-help psychological treatment or (b) are not willing to access such<br>treatments.|_C_onditional|Low|mhGAP (7):<br>DEP|
